### User

_source line 3930 · 2026-05-22T15:40:50.077Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director issued a Patch instruction: "[Patch] Round 4 审稿修复 — 83.4% framing 重写 + ASV 贡献重新定位 + Introduction 贡献层级重排" for an EMNLP 2026 via ARR submission (Long Paper, 8-page main text limit). Two specific modifications:
   
   **Modification 1 [W2 CRITICAL]**: Rewrite Introduction P2 to stop using 83.4% as a motivation sentence (overclaim flagged 4 rounds in a row). Instead: lead with K≥3 qualitative structural break, position 83.4% as quantitative evidence of secondary coefficient reversal, add qualifier "primary treatment coefficient is never reversed."
   
   **Modification 2 [W6 MAJOR]**: Reorder contributions (swap #2 ASV and #3 correction guidance), repositioning ASV as "natural corollary" rather than headline contribution. Also rewrite method.tex ASV intro paragraph to acknowledge E-value heritage while listing 3 key technical differences.
   
   Additional constraint: main text must stay within 8 pages.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - 7 DAG topologies: confounding, exposure, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door, IV
   - 83.4% sign reversal rate applies to secondary coefficients under full Dirichlet(1,1,1) prior; primary coefficient never reverses
   - ASV (Annotation Sensitivity Values) repositioned as corollary of bias taxonomy, not standalone contribution
   - 3 ASV vs E-value differences: (1) K×K→1D path requires ε-approximate monotonicity proof, (2) 3 types vs 1, (3) FWL-based correction routing
   - FWL invariance as genuine novel negative result (reviewer-validated)
   - ACL template (`\usepackage[review]{acl}`) — two-column, 11pt
   - EMNLP 2026 via ARR, 8-page main text limit

```json
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate the bias taxonomy and ASV framework through analytical derivations and Monte Carlo simulation across seven DAG topologies under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification.\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each of the seven topologies (confounding, exposure, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door, IV), we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute the analytical probability limit of the causal estimator, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with grand MAPE of 2.0\\% across 126 scenarios (18 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies, $N{=}10{,}000$). Per-topology MAPE ranges from 0.5\\% (confounding) to 5.5\\% (IV); the IV maximum reflects the Wald estimator's small-denominator variance amplification.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, 85\\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices produce bias that violates the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee. More critically, 83.4\\% of matrices cause \\emph{sign reversal} of at least one coefficient. The two failure modes are distinct: for a coefficient with true value $\\beta_1{=}1.0$, a near-permutation $C$ produces $\\hat{\\gamma}_1{=}{-}0.84$ (sign reversal, magnitude $0.84{\\times}$); for $\\beta_2{=}{-}0.5$, the same $C$ yields $\\hat{\\gamma}_2{=}{-}1.41$ (same-sign amplification, $2.83{\\times}$).\n\nThis finding persists in practically relevant subspaces. When restricting to confusion matrices with diagonal entries $\\geq 0.7$ (typical of current LLM classifiers achieving $F_1 > 0.8$), the violation rate remains 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal and 11.7\\% amplification. Even at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\%. In contrast, the $K{=}2$ control confirms that all six tested binary confusion matrices maintain the classical bias-toward-null guarantee~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing that the multi-class failure is structurally distinct from the binary case.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive of seven topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate across all sampled matrices (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}). Confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% violation and amplification but never reverse the sign.\\footnote{Consistent with \\citet{brenner1993} and \\citet{dosemeci1990}: polytomous misclassification can bias \\emph{away} from the null (amplification), but our sign-flip metric confirms the effect sign is preserved.} Collider misclassification is protective: conditioning on noisy $A^*$ induces \\emph{less} collider bias than conditioning on $A$ ($|B(C)|$ approximately non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). M-bias shows the weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation, 10.4\\% amplification).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause the Wald estimator's denominator to change sign. This rate is stable across $K{=}3,5,7$, confirming structural fragility. Unlike exposure, IV violation remains at 100\\% even at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}).\n\nAll structural patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias characteristics of $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification across 7 DAG topologies. Violation: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$ (bias exceeds true effect); Sign flip: $\\text{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\text{sign}(\\tau)$; Amplification: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$ with same sign; $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: structural sign-flip guarantee holds (\\checkmark) or not (\\ding{55}). Rates over 100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ random confusion matrices.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & \\ding{55} \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & \\checkmark \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & \\ding{55} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Envelope monotonicity.}\nThe ASV envelope (bias magnitude as a function of $\\delta$) is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for all six non-IV topologies, with worst-case step deviations below 0.06 ($<$6\\% of $\\tau$), confirmed by analytic probability limits with zero sampling noise (Appendix~\\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}). IV is excluded from Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}: the Wald estimator exhibits singularities when the first-stage coefficient passes through zero, producing unbounded deviations. These bounded deviations for non-IV topologies confirm that $\\delta$ serves as a meaningful ``dial'' controlling annotation error severity.\n\n\\paragraph{Magnitude ASV.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:asv_grid} reports magnitude ASV ranges at $\\tau{=}0.5$ with a 10\\% bias threshold, ranking from most fragile to most robust: front-door (0.064--0.387) $<$ mediation $<$ confounding $<$ IV (0.101--0.607) $<$ collider (0.338--2.028) $<$ M-bias ($\\infty$). M-bias achieves infinite ASV because annotation error only reduces the already-small spurious path.\n\n\\paragraph{Significance and sign-flip ASV.}\nAt $\\tau{=}0.05$, significance ASV achieves 100\\% power for confounding, front-door, and M-bias, but 0\\% for mediation, collider, and IV (correct behavior for attenuating topologies; Type~I error is 0\\%). For sign-flip ASV, 83.4\\% of exposure reference matrices yield $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} < 1$ (extreme sensitivity); IV sign-flip ASV ranges 1.1--6.7 (moderate-to-high). The complete ranking (Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking}, Table~\\ref{tab:asv_grid}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}) confirms IV as maximally fragile and M-bias as immune (ASV $= \\infty$).\n\n\\paragraph{$N$-sensitivity.}\nSignificance ASV exhibits a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$ (analytically computable per topology), below which all rejections are blocked. Bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}): $\\delta^* = 0.94\\,[0.60, 1.36]$ (exposure), $0.75\\,[0.44, 1.29]$ (IV).\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from three tasks: HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$). Four LLMs (Llama-4-Maverick, Qwen3-235B, DeepSeek-Chat, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct) produce 23 configurations across the three datasets under zero-shot and few-shot prompting, yielding 161 bias scenarios ($23 \\times 7$ topologies).\n\n\\paragraph{HumAID ($K{=}10$): theory--practice alignment.}\nThe empirical bias ranking matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%), exposure (9.0--17.4\\%), mediation/front-door (10.7--14.8\\%), while collider, confounding, and M-bias remain below 10\\%---consistent with the ASV vulnerability spectrum.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-$K$ comparison.}\nBias varies substantially across settings: VAST ($K{=}3$) produces 62.8\\% mean bias with IV reaching 326\\%, versus 12.2\\% for HumAID ($K{=}10$) and 32.4\\% for CivilComments ($K{=}2$). Even CivilComments configurations with $F_1{=}0.82$ (Qwen3-235B) produce 62--92\\% IV bias, confirming that classification accuracy does not predict downstream causal risk (Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}).\\footnote{VAST's near-degenerate confusion matrices (Neutral-class recall 3.6--16.1\\%) drive its extreme IV bias via the Wald estimator's small-denominator amplification.}\n\nChecking all $K{-}1$ exposure coefficients, 14 of 23 configurations (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal in at least one coefficient---all in the exposure topology. The primary coefficient ($\\beta_1$, largest magnitude) never reverses; sign flips occur exclusively in secondary coefficients with small $|\\beta|$ corresponding to rare categories. Sign reversal requires opposite-sign treatment effects across categories (Equation~\\ref{eq:exposure}); same-sign $\\beta_k$ produce only attenuation. Zero sign reversals occur across 138 non-exposure scenarios ($23 {\\times} 6$ topologies), validating the structural guarantee $K$-invariantly.\n\nASV diagnostics flag 55.4\\% of HumAID and 100\\% of CivilComments scenarios as magnitude-unsafe despite $F_1$ up to 0.82 (Appendix~\\ref{sec:asv_diagnostics}). Sign-flip patterns are preserved under logistic DGP ($|\\Delta\\,\\text{SF}_\\tau| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:logistic_dgp}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations; full results in Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}).\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nFor four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias), FWL implies that any linear transformation of the dummy matrix $\\mathbf{D}^*$ ($C^{-1}$ premultiplication, MLA, SIMEX) preserves the column space relative to $T$, leaving the treatment coefficient unchanged (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}). Naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\\% bias reduction.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean RMSE reduction (47.0\\%) but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\\%). MC-SIMEX provides gold-free correction (best for IV at 59.4\\%) but harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%). For protective topologies, correction re-exposes the original bias pathway. No single method dominates: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:case_study}).\n",
```

```json
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Setup and Notation}\n\\label{sec:notation}\n\nLet $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one. Following \\citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \\emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]\n\\label{def:nondiff}\nMisclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k, Y, \\mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis assumption is satisfied when the LLM does not observe outcome-related text during annotation---for instance, when treatment labels are assigned from source documents that exclude outcome measurements.\n\nWe study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\\cite{pearl2009} in which $A$ occupies structurally distinct causal roles: confounder, exposure, mediator, collider, M-bias variable, front-door mediator, and instrument. Each role induces a different functional relationship between $C$ and the probability limit of the standard causal estimator, yielding topology-dependent bias.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Class Bias Expressions}\n\\label{sec:bias_expressions}\n\nFor each topology, we derive the probability limit of the naive estimator that substitutes $A^*$ for $A$. Let $\\tau$ denote the target causal parameter and $\\hat{\\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounding ($A$ = confounder).}\nWhen $A$ confounds the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship, the OLS estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:confounding}\n\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(A^*) = \\bigl(\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}Z]\\bigr)^{-1}\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}X]\\,\\boldsymbol{\\beta},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $Z = [X, \\text{dummies}(A^*)]$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$ contains the true coefficients, and the target parameter $\\tau$ is the coefficient on $X$. Misclassification produces residual confounding that monotonically approaches the omitted variable bias, but the sign of $\\hat{\\tau}$ is preserved: $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$ structurally.\\footnote{This result is consistent with \\citet{brenner1993}, who showed that the adjusted estimate with misclassified polytomous confounders can be biased toward or away from the null. Both findings hold simultaneously: misclassification degrades confounding control, producing residual bias whose magnitude can exceed the correctly adjusted estimate (amplification), but the treatment effect sign is never reversed. The distinction is between \\emph{sign reversal} ($\\text{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\text{sign}(\\tau)$, which we show never occurs) and \\emph{amplification} ($|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$, which occurs universally in this topology).}\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure ($A$ = treatment variable).}\nWhen $A$ is the exposure of interest, the biased coefficient for category $j$ becomes a weighted combination of true coefficients:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:exposure}\n\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\beta}_j(A^*) = \\sum_{k=1}^{K} w_{jk}\\,\\beta_k,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $w_{jk}$ depends on the confusion matrix $C$ and the marginal distribution of $A$. Unlike the binary case, where non-differential misclassification guarantees attenuation toward the null~\\cite{brenner1993}, the $K{\\geq}3$ case permits $w_{jk}$ to assign negative weight to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, producing \\emph{sign reversal}. A near-permutation confusion matrix effectively swaps category labels, making sign reversal the dominant outcome (83.4\\% under uniform Dirichlet prior). This failure mode is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$ because a $2{\\times}2$ stochastic matrix with positive diagonal cannot reverse the sign of a single coefficient.\n\n\\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}\nWhen $A$ mediates the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mediation}\n\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}(A^*) = \\tau_{\\text{direct}} + \\bigl(1 - \\lambda(C)\\bigr)\\,\\tau_{\\text{indirect}},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$ under the sign preservation condition $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}) = \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}})$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}), with $\\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$).\n\n\\paragraph{Collider ($A$ = collider, conditioned on $A^*$).}\nConditioning on a collider $A$ of $X$ and $Y$ opens a spurious path. When we condition on $A^*$ instead, misclassification attenuates this spurious association because $A^*$ is a noisy proxy for $A$. The collider topology thus exhibits a \\emph{protective} property: annotation error reduces rather than amplifies the bias from collider stratification, with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{M-bias ($A$ = intermediate collider).}\nIn the M-shaped DAG ($U_1 \\to X$, $U_1 \\to A$, $U_2 \\to A$, $U_2 \\to Y$, $X \\to Y$), adjusting for $A$ introduces a small spurious association via the M-bias path~\\cite{greenland2003}. Misclassifying $A$ weakens this adjustment, reducing the M-bias. This topology is \\emph{immune}: $\\hat{\\tau}(A^*)$ is closer to $\\tau$ than $\\hat{\\tau}(A)$ for all non-trivial confusion matrices, yielding $\\text{ASV} = \\infty$.\n\n\\paragraph{Front-door ($A$ = front-door mediator).}\nIn the front-door DAG ($X \\to A \\to Y$ with unmeasured $U \\to X$, $U \\to Y$), the front-door estimator~\\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} uses $A$ to identify effects despite unmeasured confounding. Misclassification attenuates both $X \\to A$ and $A \\to Y$ relationships, producing moderate bias with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}\nThe Wald estimator~\\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{IV}} = \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign, causing the ratio estimator to diverge or flip. Under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\\% produce sign reversal, making IV the most fragile topology.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural sign-flip guarantee.}\n\\begin{proposition}[Sign preservation---analytic results]\n\\label{prop:signflip}\nUnder non-differential misclassification with $C_{kk} > 0$ for all $k$:\n\\emph{(a)}~Confounding preserves sign when $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau) = \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau {+} B_{\\mathrm{OV}})$, where $B_{\\mathrm{OV}} {=} \\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$ is the omitted-variable bias (Cauchy--Schwarz; Appendix~\\ref{app:confounding}).\n\\emph{(b)}~Collider preserves sign when $|\\tau| > \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g)\\operatorname{Var}(h)}/\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T{\\mid}A)]$ (Berkson attenuation; Appendix~\\ref{app:collider}).\n\\emph{(c)}~Exposure can produce sign reversal for $K {\\geq} 3$, even with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ (counterexample in Appendix~\\ref{app:exposure}).\n\\end{proposition}\n\nOur theoretical results establish a three-tier hierarchy: (i)~analytically proved (Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}(a,b), Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}), (ii)~numerically verified with zero violations across 100{,}000 matrices (mediation, M-bias, front-door sign preservation), and (iii)~conjectured with $\\varepsilon$-approximate bounds (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}).\n\n\\paragraph{Numerical Verification 1} (Mediation, M-bias, front-door).\nSign preservation holds under topology-specific conditions: mediation requires $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}) {=} \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}})$; front-door requires $\\operatorname{sign}(\\lambda\\alpha_U) {=} \\operatorname{sign}(\\lambda\\alpha_U {+} \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)))$; M-bias is attenuating for all $C$. Verified across 100{,}000 random matrices with zero violations (Appendix~\\ref{app:conditions}); analytic proofs for general $K$ remain open.\n\nThese conditions involve true DGP parameters, which in practice must be estimated. The ASV framework (§\\ref{sec:asv}) provides a complementary assessment that does not require knowledge of the exact sufficient conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Annotation Sensitivity Values}\n\\label{sec:asv}\n\nOur bias taxonomy and FWL invariance results naturally motivate a practical question: how much annotation error can a study tolerate before conclusions change? We define Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs) for this purpose, drawing on the E-value framework~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} but with three key differences: (1)~E-values parametrize a scalar $\\Gamma$ that is naturally monotone; ASVs map a $K{\\times}K$ matrix to a 1D path $C(\\delta)$, requiring proof of $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}); (2)~three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) address topology-dependent failure modes; and (3)~ASVs combine with FWL invariance to route practitioners to topology-appropriate corrections.\n\n\\paragraph{Parameterization.}\nWe parameterize the confusion matrix as $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, where $C_0$ is a reference confusion matrix (e.g., estimated from a validation set) and $\\delta \\geq 0$ controls the severity of misclassification. At $\\delta{=}0$, classification is perfect; at $\\delta{=}1$, classification matches $C_0$. Non-negativity of $C(\\delta)$ requires $\\delta \\leq \\delta_{\\max} = \\min_j \\frac{1}{1 - (C_0)_{jj}}$. Since $(C_0)_{jj} \\in [0,1]$, we have $\\delta_{\\max} \\geq 1$ for all valid $C_0$, ensuring the full range $[0,1]$ is always feasible; all ASV computations are constrained to $[0, \\delta_{\\max}]$.\n\\paragraph{Three ASV types.}\nWe define three complementary measures: (1)~\\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{mag}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta)) - \\tau| > \\epsilon\\}$---analogous to E-values; (2)~\\textbf{Significance ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sig}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta))| < z_{\\alpha/2} \\cdot SE(N)\\}$---relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} = \\min\\{\\delta : \\text{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\text{sign}(\\tau)\\}$---exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity---confounding and mediation]\n\\label{thm:monotone}\nFor confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$ for some scalar $\\lambda$) under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\\delta \\in [0, \\delta_{\\max}]$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\n\\emph{Proof sketch.} Both topologies reduce to $B(C(\\delta)) {=} \\lambda V_W(\\delta)/(V_W(\\delta) {+} \\sigma^2)$ where $V_W(\\delta) {=} \\operatorname{Var}(g) {-} \\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g{\\mid}A^*_\\delta])$ and $\\sigma^2$ is a topology-specific constant ($\\sigma_T^2$ for confounding, $\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T{\\mid}A)]$ for mediation). As $\\delta$ increases, $A^*$ becomes a noisier channel for $A$, so $\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g{\\mid}A^*_\\delta])$ decreases by the data processing inequality, increasing $V_W$. Since $x/(x{+}c)$ is increasing for $c{>}0$, $|B|$ is non-decreasing (full proofs in Appendix~\\ref{app:monotonicity_proof} and~\\ref{par:med_monotone}). For general (non-proportional) effects, numerical verification across 10{,}000 random matrices confirms $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity with $\\varepsilon < 0.02$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:monotonicity_proof}).\n\n\\begin{conjecture}[$\\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]\n\\label{conj:monotone}\nFor the four remaining non-IV topologies (exposure, collider, M-bias, front-door) under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$: exposure and front-door are $\\varepsilon$-approximately non-decreasing in $\\delta$, and collider and M-bias (protective topologies) are $\\varepsilon$-approximately non-increasing in $\\delta$, with $\\varepsilon < 0.06$ ($<$6\\% of $\\tau$). Mediation envelope monotonicity is proved for proportional effects (Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}) and verified numerically for the general case with $\\varepsilon < 0.02$. Verified across 10{,}000 random matrices per topology and $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ using analytic probability limits (Appendix~\\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}).\n\\end{conjecture}\n\nThe IV topology is excluded from Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}: the Wald estimator exhibits singularities when the first-stage coefficient passes through zero, producing unbounded deviations. Magnitude ASV remains well-defined away from these singularities.\n\nWhen strict monotonicity holds only approximately ($\\varepsilon < 0.06$), the reported $\\delta^*$ may differ from the true threshold by at most $\\varepsilon/\\eta$ in $\\delta$-units---negligible at the recommended $\\eta = 10\\%$. Topology rankings are preserved across all tested configurations.\n\nBinarization of $K{=}3$ annotations introduces additional topology-dependent bias (Appendix~\\ref{app:binarization}).\n",
        "numLines": 102,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 102
      }
    },
```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology across seven canonical topologies. The classical binary guarantee breaks at $K \\geq 3$: exposure and IV topologies produce sign reversal, while five topologies maintain a structural guarantee. The ASV framework reveals a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune), and matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four topologies due to FWL invariance. Empirical validation with 23 real LLM configurations confirms: the primary treatment effect never reverses, but 61\\% of exposure configurations reverse a secondary coefficient. Differential misclassification and correlated multi-LLM errors are natural extensions. Code for ASV computation and all experiments will be publicly released.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur framework assumes non-differential misclassification ($P(A^*|A, Y, \\mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*|A)$), which holds when the LLM does not observe $Y$ but may be violated when it accesses $Y$-correlated information---e.g., outcome-conditional prompting, few-shot demonstrations encoding outcome--treatment associations, or retrieval-augmented annotation carrying outcome signal (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}). The framework assumes a correctly specified causal DAG; when the topology is uncertain, practitioners should compute ASV under candidate DAGs and report the most conservative. Closed-form expressions are derived for $K{=}3$ and verified at $K{=}5,7$ under linear structural equations; extensions to arbitrary $K$ and nonlinear models (Appendix~\\ref{sec:logistic_dgp}) remain open. We address misclassification of $A$ by a single annotator; outcome misclassification and correlated multi-annotator settings~\\cite{kim_garg2025} introduce additional bias channels not captured here.\n",

        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 9
      }
```

```json
{

      "type": "text",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\\% for smaller models; these errors are highly correlated across models (60\\% agreement when both err)~\\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \\emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.\n\nThe transition at $K{\\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ to preserve coefficient signs---an algebraic property of the $2{\\times}2$ matrix dimension, not of a specific error rate~\\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee \\emph{vanishes} at $K{\\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal even when every diagonal entry exceeds $0.5$~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. This structural break is quantifiable: under a uniform Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior spanning the full parameter space, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient (0\\% at $K{=}2$); under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), 2.1\\% do so. Critically, the primary treatment coefficient is never reversed---a structural property we prove in Section~\\ref{sec:method}.\n\nStandard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\\% to 42.1\\%), illustrating that \\emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \\citet{dosemeci1990} and \\citet{veirod_laake2001} noted the possibility of polytomous sign reversal; \\citet{brenner1993} identified amplification for polytomous confounders; \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology. No prior work systematically characterizes how the \\emph{same} confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across causal designs.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification, prove sign preservation for confounding and collider, verify it numerically for mediation, M-bias, and front-door, and show that exposure and IV can produce sign reversal. Binarization amplifies the gap: collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary introduces additional bias varying 154$\\times$ across topologies.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate---a negative result overlooked in the annotation correction literature. PPI++ achieves 47.0\\% RMSE reduction but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\\%).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} As a corollary of the bias taxonomy, we define three complementary ASV types---magnitude, significance, and sign-flip---revealing a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile, 52.6\\% sign reversal) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 19,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 19
      }
    },
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). ```text
```
```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Large language models are increasingly used as annotators in computational social science, yet annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the confusion matrix structure and the causal DAG topology. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical topologies under $K$-class non-differential misclassification and show that $K {\geq} 3$ introduces a structural possibility of sign reversal absent in the binary case. We prove sign preservation for confounding and collider, and verify it numerically for mediation, M-bias, and front-door; exposure and IV topologies can reverse the treatment effect sign. Under realistic LLM accuracy, sign reversal of non-dominant coefficients occurs under exposure; under empirical LLM matrices (diagonal 0.45--0.81), sign reversal is confined to exposure; IV is fragile under uniform Dirichlet priors but exhibits zero reversals under empirical LLM matrices. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), with proved ($K{=}2$) and numerically verified ($K{\geq}3$) monotonicity, yielding a vulnerability ranking from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune), and show that matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective under linear regression for four topologies due to Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance. Topology-aware analysis is necessary for valid causal inference with LLM-generated annotations.
3	\end{abstract}
4
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}
5	The foundations are established in \citet{carroll2006}, \citet{gustafson2004}, \citet{buonaccorsi2010}, and \citet{bound2001measurement}, with starred-node DAGs formalized by \citet{hernan_cole2009} and DAG-based correction theory developed by \citet{kuroki_pearl2014}. For binary variables, non-differential misclassification biases toward the null~\cite{brenner1993, ogburn_vanderweele2012}, with \citet{neuhaus1999} quantifying the attenuation and efficiency loss in binary regression under known misclassification rates; but this fails for polytomous exposures~\cite{dosemeci1990, jurek2005, veirod_laake2001}. \citet{edwards_lessler_naimi2021} showed dependent misclassification can bias in either direction, and \citet{shu_yi2019a, shu_yi2019b} develop weighted estimators that jointly handle mismeasured covariates and misclassified outcomes under IPW---a concern that also drives mediation analyses with mismeasured mediators~\cite{imai_keele_tingley2010, valeri_vanderweele2013, valeri_vanderweele2014}. \citet{schennach2020mismeasured} addresses general nonclassical measurement error in regression; \citet{miles2024measurement} construct instrumental variables for measurement-error-robust estimation; \citet{wei2025multiply} develop multiply robust estimators under misclassified treatment. All analyze a single DAG topology; ours is the first to characterize how the \emph{same} confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across seven topologies.
6	
7	\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation.}
8	\citet{grimmer2022} and \citet{feder_etal2022} provide foundations for text-as-data and causal inference in NLP. \citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to classifier error; \citet{teblunthuis2024} gave the most complete binary treatment ($K{=}2$, four DAGs); \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only confounding. On annotation quality, \citet{gilardi2023chatgpt} and \citet{ziems2024can} document the rapid adoption of LLM annotators (surveyed by \citealt{tan2024llm}), \citet{baumann2025} found 31\% incorrect conclusions, \citet{pangakis_wolken_fasching2023} demonstrated that automated annotations require task-specific validation, \citet{cheng_mayya_sedoc2024} propose structured approaches to reduce LLM measurement error, \citet{variance_aware_llm2026} develop variance-aware annotation protocols, \citet{hidden_measurement_error2026} characterizes hidden measurement error in LLM pipelines, and \citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\% error correlation across models. \citet{audinet2025} benchmark debiasing; \citet{keith_jensen_oconnor2020} survey the broader landscape. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose quality without connecting it to causal validity.
9	
10	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis and correction methods.}
11	Our ASV framework draws on the sensitivity analysis without assumptions of \citet{ding_vanderweele2016}, E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017, mathur2018}, the robustness value of \citet{cinelli2020making}, and the mediation sensitivity analysis of \citet{imai_keele_yamamoto2010}, and text-based causal inference methods~\cite{pryzant_etal2021}, but parametrizes annotation error severity rather than omitted variable strength or sequential ignorability violations. The quantitative bias analysis framework of \citet{lash2009} provides a complementary epidemiologic perspective, and \citet{mahajan2006} establishes identification of regression models with misclassified regressors via instrumental variables. The unified measurement-error and missing-data framework of \citet{blackwell_honaker_king2017} similarly motivates treating annotation noise as a first-class object of inference. For correction, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023} and DSL~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} are topology-agnostic; \citet{zrnic_candes2024} extend PPI to gold-free settings, \citet{demirel2024prediction} generalize prediction-powered inference to causal parameters across populations, and \citet{cadei2025} adapt it to treatment-effect targets via a deconfounded risk objective, and \citet{gligoric2025} leverage LLM confidence scores. Our contribution reveals that this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is itself topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective for four topologies. Bayesian measurement error models~\cite{richardson1993bayesian, gustafson2004} and EM imputation offer alternatives that bypass FWL invariance.
12	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01AezPXWL7tiJkc9MmyvzYRk`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Large language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors (equivalently, misclassification) from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\% for smaller models; these errors are highly correlated across models (60\% agreement when both err)~\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.
5	
6	The transition at $K{\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with positive diagonal to preserve coefficient signs---an algebraic property of the $2{\times}2$ matrix dimension~\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee \emph{vanishes} at $K{\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal even under high accuracy~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. Under a uniform Dirichlet prior, 83.4\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient; under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\geq 0.7$), 2.1\% still do. The primary treatment coefficient, however, is never reversed in any tested configuration---an empirical regularity we discuss in Section~\ref{sec:method}.
7	
8	Standard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\% to 42.1\%). $F_1$ shows no significant rank correlation with causal bias across 56 HumAID scenarios (Spearman $\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$), confirming that \emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \citet{dosemeci1990} and \citet{veirod_laake2001} noted the possibility of polytomous sign reversal; \citet{brenner1993} identified amplification for polytomous confounders; \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology. We present the first unified analysis of \emph{LLM-generated} multi-class annotation error propagation across seven canonical DAG topologies \emph{in linear SEMs}, extending prior single-topology analyses~\cite{brenner1993, teblunthuis2024}. Our analytical predictions match Monte Carlo estimates within 1.6\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.
9	
10	We address these gaps with three contributions:
11	
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification in linear structural equation models, prove sign preservation for confounding and collider, verify it numerically for mediation, M-bias, and front-door, and show that exposure and IV can produce sign reversal. Binarization amplifies the gap: collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary introduces additional bias varying $154{\times}$ across topologies (Appendix~\ref{app:binarization}).
14	
15	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} As a corollary of the bias taxonomy, we define three complementary ASV diagnostics---magnitude, significance, and sign-flip---revealing a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile; 52.6\% sign reversal under random Dirichlet prior, though zero reversals occur under empirical LLM matrices) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$).
16	
17	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance} (a systematic benchmark, not a new correction method). We evaluate five correction methods and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction for four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate---a negative result overlooked in the annotation correction literature. PPI++ achieves 47.0\% RMSE reduction but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\%).
18	\end{enumerate}
19	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup and Notation}
5	\label{sec:notation}
6	
7	Let $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one. Following \citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.
8	
9	\paragraph{Notation conventions.}
10	Our analysis characterizes systematic bias---the value an estimator converges to as $N \to \infty$ (the probability limit, or plim)---rather than finite-sample variance. The Frisch--Waugh--Lovell (FWL) theorem guarantees that each regression coefficient equals the coefficient from a simple regression after residualizing all other variables; this is why matrix-based corrections fail for covariate topologies (Section~\ref{sec:correction}): replacing the mismeasured variable with a corrected version does not change the residualized relationship. The Wald estimator (ratio of reduced-form to first-stage effects) diverges under weak instruments; Dirichlet priors over probability vectors represent uncertainty over misclassification patterns. Table~\ref{tab:notation} (Appendix~\ref{app:notation_table}) collects all notation.
11	
12	\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]
13	\label{def:nondiff}
14	Misclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k, Y, \mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.
15	\end{definition}
16	
17	This holds when the LLM classifies without observing the outcome---the typical CSS setup~\cite{ziems2024can}; topic-stratified confusion matrices confirm cross-group stability (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_verify}). Violations arise when pretraining data encodes $A$--$Y$ associations; our stress test (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}) shows $<$3~pp degradation under uniform $\varepsilon{=}0.05$ violations, but $\varepsilon{=}0.01$ structured departure produces sign reversal in 14/16 $K{\geq}3$ exposure configurations (Appendix~\ref{sec:realistic_epsilon}). Practitioners can test this by comparing $C$ across outcome strata; in typical CSS pipelines where annotation is performed without access to $Y$, non-differentiality is structurally ensured~\cite{ziems2024can}.
18	
19	We study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\cite{pearl2009, hernan2020causal} in which $A$ occupies structurally distinct causal roles: confounder, exposure, mediator, collider, M-bias variable, front-door mediator, and instrument. Throughout, we derive probability limits under \emph{linear} structural equation models~\cite{carroll2006}, the standard framework for exact characterization of topology-dependent bias; robustness under logistic regression~\cite{stefanski_carroll1985} is verified in Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp} and~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}.
20	
21	\subsection{Multi-Class Bias Expressions}
22	\label{sec:bias_expressions}
23	
24	Intuitively, misclassification redistributes probability mass across categories; the downstream effect depends on whether this redistribution aligns with or opposes the true causal structure.
25	
26	For each topology, we derive the $\text{plim}$\footnote{The probability limit (plim) is the value to which an estimator converges as sample size $N \to \infty$; it characterizes systematic bias separately from sampling variability.} of the naive estimator that substitutes $A^*$ for $A$. Let $\tau$ denote the target causal parameter---its precise definition is topology-specific (e.g., the coefficient on $X$ for confounding, the category-specific $\beta_j$ for exposure, or the direct effect $\tau_{\text{direct}}$ for mediation)---and $\hat{\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.
27	
28	\paragraph{Confounding ($A$ = confounder).}
29	When $A$ confounds the $X{\to}Y$ relationship, the OLS estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit
30	\begin{equation}
31	\label{eq:confounding}
32	\text{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(A^*) = \bigl(\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}Z]\bigr)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}X]\,\boldsymbol{\beta},
33	\end{equation}
34	where $Z = [X, \text{dummies}(A^*)]$, $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ contains the true coefficients, and the target parameter $\tau$ is the coefficient on $X$. Misclassification produces residual confounding that monotonically approaches the omitted variable bias, but the sign of $\hat{\tau}$ is preserved: $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$ structurally.\footnote{Consistent with \citet{brenner1993}: misclassification degrades confounding control, producing amplification ($|\hat{\tau}| > |\tau|$, universal in this topology) but never sign reversal ($\text{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \text{sign}(\tau)$).}
35	
36	\paragraph{Exposure ($A$ = treatment variable).}
37	When $A$ is the exposure of interest, the biased coefficient for category $j$ becomes a weighted combination of true coefficients:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\label{eq:exposure}
40	\text{plim}\;\hat{\beta}_j(A^*) = \sum_{k=1}^{K} w_{jk}\,\beta_k,
41	\end{equation}
42	where $w_{jk}$ depends on the confusion matrix $C$ and the population marginal $\pi_k = P(A{=}k)$. Unlike the binary case, where non-differential misclassification guarantees attenuation toward the null~\cite{brenner1993}, the $K{\geq}3$ case permits $w_{jk}$ to assign negative weight to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, producing \emph{sign reversal}. A near-permutation confusion matrix effectively swaps category labels, making sign reversal the dominant outcome (83.4\% under uniform Dirichlet prior). This failure mode is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$ because a $2{\times}2$ stochastic matrix with positive diagonal cannot reverse the sign of a single coefficient.
43	
44	\begin{figure*}[t]
45	\centering
46	\includegraphics[width=0.92\textwidth,height=0.18\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_bias_distribution.pdf}
47	\vspace{-6pt}
48	\caption{Bias distributions under 100{,}000 random $K{=}3$ confusion matrices. Red = sign reversal. Exposure: 83.4\% sign reversal; IV: 52.6\%.}
49	\label{fig:bias_distribution}
50	\end{figure*}
51	
52	\paragraph{Numerical Verification 2: Primary coefficient preservation.}
53	Across 23 empirical LLM configurations, the primary coefficient (the category with the largest true effect $|\beta_k|$) never reverses sign, while 61\% reverse a secondary (non-dominant) coefficient---confirmed under 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices with diagonal ${\geq}\,0.7$ (0\% primary flip; Appendix~\ref{app:exposure}).
54	
55	\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}
56	When $A$ mediates the $X{\to}Y$ relationship~\cite{vanderweele2015explanation}, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit
57	\begin{equation}
58	\label{eq:mediation}
59	\text{plim}\;\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}(A^*) = \tau_{\text{direct}} + \bigl(1 - \lambda(C)\bigr)\,\tau_{\text{indirect}},
60	\end{equation}
61	where $\lambda(C) \in [0,1]$ under proportional effects (Appendix~\ref{app:proof}, Proposition~\ref{prop:lambda_bound}), with $\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$).
62	
63	\paragraph{Collider, M-bias, and front-door.}
64	Three topologies exhibit \emph{protective} or \emph{immune} behavior. \textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates the spurious path, so annotation error reduces collider bias ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$). \textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\cite{greenland2003}, misclassifying $A$ weakens the M-bias path; this topology is immune ($\text{ASV} = \infty$). \textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} is attenuated in both $X \to A$ and $A \to Y$ stages, producing moderate bias with $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$.
65	
66	\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}
67	The Wald estimator~\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\hat{\tau}_{\text{IV}} = \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign, causing the ratio estimator to diverge or flip. Under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\% produce sign reversal, making IV the most fragile topology (detailed analysis including the Dirichlet subspace geometry in Appendix~\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}).
68	
69	\paragraph{Structural sign-flip guarantee.}
70	\begin{proposition}[Sign preservation---analytic results]
71	\label{prop:signflip}
72	Under non-differential misclassification with $C_{kk} > 0$ for all $k$:
73	\emph{(a)}~Confounding preserves sign when $\operatorname{sign}(\tau) = \operatorname{sign}(\tau {+} B_{\mathrm{OV}})$, where $B_{\mathrm{OV}} {=} \operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))/\operatorname{Var}(T)$ is the omitted-variable bias (Cauchy--Schwarz; Appendix~\ref{app:confounding}).
74	\emph{(b)}~Collider preserves sign when $|\tau| > \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(g)\operatorname{Var}(h)}/\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(T{\mid}A)]$ (Berkson attenuation; Appendix~\ref{app:collider}); empirically verified across 100{,}000 matrices with zero violations.
75	\emph{(c)}~Exposure can produce sign reversal for $K {\geq} 3$, even with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ (counterexample in Appendix~\ref{app:exposure}).
76	\end{proposition}
77	
78	Results follow a three-tier hierarchy: analytically proved (Proposition~\ref{prop:signflip}, Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}), numerically verified with zero violations (Observation~\ref{prop:monotone_general}), and conjectured with $\varepsilon$-approximate bounds (Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}).
79	
80	\paragraph{Numerical Verification 1: Non-exposure sign preservation.}
81	Sign preservation holds under topology-specific conditions (Table~\ref{tab:sign_conditions}, Appendix~\ref{app:conditions}), verified across 100{,}000 random matrices with zero violations; analytic proofs for general $K$ remain open. Since these conditions involve true DGP parameters, the ASV framework (§\ref{sec:asv}) provides a complementary assessment.
82	
83	\subsection{Annotation Sensitivity Values}
84	\label{sec:asv}
85	
86	How much annotation error can a study tolerate before conclusions change? We adapt the sensitivity analysis paradigm---E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} (the minimum confounding strength needed to explain away an observed effect), partial-$R^2$ bounds~\cite{cinelli2020making}, coefficient stability~\cite{oster2019}---to $K{\times}K$ annotation error, defining Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs). Our approach: (1)~prove $\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity along a 1D path $C(\delta)$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}), reducing the $K^2$-dimensional problem to a scalar threshold; (2)~define three ASV diagnostics addressing topology-dependent failure modes; (3)~combine ASVs with FWL invariance to route practitioners to topology-appropriate corrections.
87	
88	\paragraph{Parameterization.}
89	We parameterize the confusion matrix as $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, where $C_0$ is a reference confusion matrix and $\delta \in [0,1]$ controls severity ($\delta{=}0$: perfect; $\delta{=}1$: matches $C_0$). Non-negativity requires $\delta \leq \delta_{\max} = \min_j 1/(1 - (C_0)_{jj}) \geq 1$; we recommend bootstrapping $C_0$ and reporting ASV confidence intervals (Appendix~\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}).
90	\paragraph{Three ASV types.}
91	We define three complementary measures: (1)~\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{mag}} = \min\{\delta : |\text{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta)) - \tau| > \epsilon\}$---analogous to E-values; (2)~\textbf{Significance ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sig}} = \min\{\delta : |\text{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta))| < z_{\alpha/2} \cdot SE(N)\}$---relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sf}} = \min\{\delta : \text{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \text{sign}(\tau)\}$---exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.
92	
93	\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity---$K{=}2$]
94	\label{thm:monotone}
95	For $K{=}2$ confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \lambda g$ for some scalar $\lambda$) under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$ with $(C_0)_{jj} \geq \tfrac{1}{2}$, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\delta \in [0,1]$.
96	\end{theorem}
97	
98	The proof (Appendix~\ref{app:monotonicity_proof},~\ref{par:med_monotone}) exploits perspective function convexity; $(C_0)_{jj}{\geq}\tfrac{1}{2}$ excludes degenerate matrices. Proportional effects ($f = \lambda g$) holds when a single latent factor drives both paths. The assumption is testable by regressing $f(A)$ on $g(A)$; it is plausible in NLP tasks with a single quality dimension but weakens under heterogeneous quality (Table~\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}). Under NLP-realistic DGPs, the out-of-bound rate drops to 0.07\% with zero sign failures; even under non-proportional effects with 23 real LLM matrices, sign preservation remains ${\geq}97.3$\% (Tables~\ref{tab:proportional_effects},~\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}). When $\lambda(C) \notin [0,1]$, Proposition~\ref{prop:conf_gen} provides a complementary bound.
99	
100	\begin{observation}[Envelope monotonicity---$K{\geq}3$]
101	\label{prop:monotone_general}
102	For $K{\geq}3$ under the same conditions, numerical verification across 20{,}000 random $(C_0,p,g)$ triples per $K$ shows worst-case deviations are negligible ($\leq 0.013$; Table~\ref{tab:conjecture_verify} in Appendix~\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}).
103	\end{observation}
104	
105	\begin{conjecture}[$\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]
106	\label{conj:monotone}
107	For non-IV topologies under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is monotonic up to $\varepsilon < 0.06$ deviations under default DGP parameters (collider/M-bias non-increasing, others non-decreasing; see Appendix~\ref{sec:conjecture_verify} for DGP-dependent bounds). Verified across 10{,}000 random matrices per topology for each $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ (210{,}000 total), $\tau \in \{0.1, 0.5, 1.0, 2.0, 5.0\}$.
108	\end{conjecture}
109	
110	\begin{remark}[Structural distinctions from E-values]
111	\label{rem:evalue}
112	ASV diagnostics differ from E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} in three respects (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_comparison}, Appendix):
113	\emph{(i)~Topology dependence}: the same $C$ preserves sign under confounding but reverses it under exposure (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}); E-values treat all confounding identically.
114	\emph{(ii)~Sign-flip diagnostic}: sign-flip ASV $\delta^*_{\mathrm{sf}}$ has no E-value counterpart.
115	\emph{(iii)~Parametric structure}: ASV reduces $K^2$ dimensions to one via $C(\delta)$, but $B(\delta)$ is topology-specific and can be non-monotone (IV). E-values optimize over $[1,\infty)$; ASVs search $\Delta_{K-1}^K$---the space of column-stochastic matrices---a $(K{-}1)^2$-dimensional convex body whose structure makes optimization harder as $K$ grows.
116	\end{remark}
117	
118	Under alternating-sign effects ($\boldsymbol{\beta}{=}[0,\,1.0,\,-0.5]$), magnitude ASV is 0.09 for exposure, 0.20 for IV, 0.35 for confounding, and $\infty$ for M-bias (Table~\ref{tab:asv_stability})---the same annotator quality produces a $4{\times}$ difference in tolerable error, determined entirely by DAG topology. This topology-dependent diagnostic is unavailable in E-value frameworks, which treat all confounding structures identically.
119	
120	For $K{=}2$, Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone} guarantees exact monotonicity; for $K{\geq}3$, monotonicity rests on numerical evidence (Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}). Sign-flip ASV $< 1$ flags sign-reversal-prone pairs; magnitude ASV $< \delta_{\text{observed}}$ indicates bias exceeds tolerance (Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}). The safe/fragile partition is stable across DGP parameterizations; within-group ordering depends on coefficient structure (Appendix~\ref{sec:asv_stability}). The worst-case monotonicity deviation ($\varepsilon \leq 0.013$; Table~\ref{tab:conjecture_verify}) is two orders of magnitude smaller than inter-topology ASV gaps (spanning 0.09 to $\infty$), ensuring that the vulnerability ranking is robust to approximate monotonicity.
121
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Setup and Notation}\n\\label{sec:notation}\n\nLet $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one. Following \\citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \\emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.\n\n\\paragraph{Notation conventions.}\nOur analysis characterizes systematic bias---the value an estimator converges to as $N \\to \\infty$ (the probability limit, or plim)---rather than finite-sample variance. The Frisch--Waugh--Lovell (FWL) theorem guarantees that each regression coefficient equals the coefficient from a simple regression after residualizing all other variables; this is why matrix-based corrections fail for covariate topologies (Section~\\ref{sec:correction}): replacing the mismeasured variable with a corrected version does not change the residualized relationship. The Wald estimator (ratio of reduced-form to first-stage effects) diverges under weak instruments; Dirichlet priors over probability vectors represent uncertainty over misclassification patterns. Table~\\ref{tab:notation} (Appendix~\\ref{app:notation_table}) collects all notation.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]\n\\label{def:nondiff}\nMisclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k, Y, \\mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis holds when the LLM classifies without observing the outcome---the typical CSS setup~\\cite{ziems2024can}; topic-stratified confusion matrices confirm cross-group stability (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_verify}). Violations arise when pretraining data encodes $A$--$Y$ associations; our stress test (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}) shows $<$3~pp degradation under uniform $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ violations, but $\\varepsilon{=}0.01$ structured departure produces sign reversal in 14/16 $K{\\geq}3$ exposure configurations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:realistic_epsilon}). Practitioners can test this by comparing $C$ across outcome strata; in typical CSS pipelines where annotation is performed without access to $Y$, non-differentiality is structurally ensured~\\cite{ziems2024can}.\n\nWe study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\\cite{pearl2009, hernan2020causal} in which $A$ occupies structurally distinct causal roles: confounder, exposure, mediator, collider, M-bias variable, front-door mediator, and instrument. Throughout, we derive probability limits under \\emph{linear} structural equation models~\\cite{carroll2006}, the standard framework for exact characterization of topology-dependent bias; robustness under logistic regression~\\cite{stefanski_carroll1985} is verified in Appendix~\\ref{sec:logistic_dgp} and~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Class Bias Expressions}\n\\label{sec:bias_expressions}\n\nIntuitively, misclassification redistributes probability mass across categories; the downstream effect depends on whether this redistribution aligns with or opposes the true causal structure.\n\nFor each topology, we derive the $\\text{plim}$\\footnote{The probability limit (plim) is the value to which an estimator converges as sample size $N \\to \\infty$; it characterizes systematic bias separately from sampling variability.} of the naive estimator that substitutes $A^*$ for $A$. Let $\\tau$ denote the target causal parameter---its precise definition is topology-specific (e.g., the coefficient on $X$ for confounding, the category-specific $\\beta_j$ for exposure, or the direct effect $\\tau_{\\text{direct}}$ for mediation)---and $\\hat{\\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounding ($A$ = confounder).}\nWhen $A$ confounds the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship, the OLS estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:confounding}\n\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(A^*) = \\bigl(\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}Z]\\bigr)^{-1}\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}X]\\,\\boldsymbol{\\beta},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $Z = [X, \\text{dummies}(A^*)]$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$ contains the true coefficients, and the target parameter $\\tau$ is the coefficient on $X$. Misclassification produces residual confounding that monotonically approaches the omitted variable bias, but the sign of $\\hat{\\tau}$ is preserved: $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$ structurally.\\footnote{Consistent with \\citet{brenner1993}: misclassification degrades confounding control, producing amplification ($|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$, universal in this topology) but never sign reversal ($\\text{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\text{sign}(\\tau)$).}\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure ($A$ = treatment variable).}\nWhen $A$ is the exposure of interest, the biased coefficient for category $j$ becomes a weighted combination of true coefficients:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:exposure}\n\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\beta}_j(A^*) = \\sum_{k=1}^{K} w_{jk}\\,\\beta_k,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $w_{jk}$ depends on the confusion matrix $C$ and the population marginal $\\pi_k = P(A{=}k)$. Unlike the binary case, where non-differential misclassification guarantees attenuation toward the null~\\cite{brenner1993}, the $K{\\geq}3$ case permits $w_{jk}$ to assign negative weight to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, producing \\emph{sign reversal}. A near-permutation confusion matrix effectively swaps category labels, making sign reversal the dominant outcome (83.4\\% under uniform Dirichlet prior). This failure mode is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$ because a $2{\\times}2$ stochastic matrix with positive diagonal cannot reverse the sign of a single coefficient.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.92\\textwidth,height=0.18\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_bias_distribution.pdf}\n\\vspace{-6pt}\n\\caption{Bias distributions under 100{,}000 random $K{=}3$ confusion matrices. Red = sign reversal. Exposure: 83.4\\% sign reversal; IV: 52.6\\%.}\n\\label{fig:bias_distribution}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\paragraph{Numerical Verification 2: Primary coefficient preservation.}\nAcross 23 empirical LLM configurations, the primary coefficient (the category with the largest true effect $|\\beta_k|$) never reverses sign, while 61\\% reverse a secondary (non-dominant) coefficient---confirmed under 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices with diagonal ${\\geq}\\,0.7$ (0\\% primary flip; Appendix~\\ref{app:exposure}).\n\n\\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}\nWhen $A$ mediates the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship~\\cite{vanderweele2015explanation}, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mediation}\n\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}(A^*) = \\tau_{\\text{direct}} + \\bigl(1 - \\lambda(C)\\bigr)\\,\\tau_{\\text{indirect}},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$ under proportional effects (Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}, Proposition~\\ref{prop:lambda_bound}), with $\\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$).\n\n\\paragraph{Collider, M-bias, and front-door.}\nThree topologies exhibit \\emph{protective} or \\emph{immune} behavior. \\textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates the spurious path, so annotation error reduces collider bias ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$). \\textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\\cite{greenland2003}, misclassifying $A$ weakens the M-bias path; this topology is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). \\textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} is attenuated in both $X \\to A$ and $A \\to Y$ stages, producing moderate bias with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}\nThe Wald estimator~\\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{IV}} = \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign, causing the ratio estimator to diverge or flip. Under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\\% produce sign reversal, making IV the most fragile topology (detailed analysis including the Dirichlet subspace geometry in Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}).\n\n\\paragraph{Structural sign-flip guarantee.}\n\\begin{proposition}[Sign preservation---analytic results]\n\\label{prop:signflip}\nUnder non-differential misclassification with $C_{kk} > 0$ for all $k$:\n\\emph{(a)}~Confounding preserves sign when $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau) = \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau {+} B_{\\mathrm{OV}})$, where $B_{\\mathrm{OV}} {=} \\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$ is the omitted-variable bias (Cauchy--Schwarz; Appendix~\\ref{app:confounding}).\n\\emph{(b)}~Collider preserves sign when $|\\tau| > \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g)\\operatorname{Var}(h)}/\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T{\\mid}A)]$ (Berkson attenuation; Appendix~\\ref{app:collider}); empirically verified across 100{,}000 matrices with zero violations.\n\\emph{(c)}~Exposure can produce sign reversal for $K {\\geq} 3$, even with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ (counterexample in Appendix~\\ref{app:exposure}).\n\\end{proposition}\n\nResults follow a three-tier hierarchy: analytically proved (Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}, Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}), numerically verified with zero violations (Observation~\\ref{prop:monotone_general}), and conjectured with $\\varepsilon$-approximate bounds (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}).\n\n\\paragraph{Numerical Verification 1: Non-exposure sign preservation.}\nSign preservation holds under topology-specific conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:sign_conditions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:conditions}), verified across 100{,}000 random matrices with zero violations; analytic proofs for general $K$ remain open. Since these conditions involve true DGP parameters, the ASV framework (§\\ref{sec:asv}) provides a complementary assessment.\n\n\\subsection{Annotation Sensitivity Values}\n\\label{sec:asv}\n\nHow much annotation error can a study tolerate before conclusions change? We adapt the sensitivity analysis paradigm---E-values~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} (the minimum confounding strength needed to explain away an observed effect), partial-$R^2$ bounds~\\cite{cinelli2020making}, coefficient stability~\\cite{oster2019}---to $K{\\times}K$ annotation error, defining Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs). Our approach: (1)~prove $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity along a 1D path $C(\\delta)$ (Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}), reducing the $K^2$-dimensional problem to a scalar threshold; (2)~define three ASV diagnostics addressing topology-dependent failure modes; (3)~combine ASVs with FWL invariance to route practitioners to topology-appropriate corrections.\n\n\\paragraph{Parameterization.}\nWe parameterize the confusion matrix as $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, where $C_0$ is a reference confusion matrix and $\\delta \\in [0,1]$ controls severity ($\\delta{=}0$: perfect; $\\delta{=}1$: matches $C_0$). Non-negativity requires $\\delta \\leq \\delta_{\\max} = \\min_j 1/(1 - (C_0)_{jj}) \\geq 1$; we recommend bootstrapping $C_0$ and reporting ASV confidence intervals (Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}).\n\\paragraph{Three ASV types.}\nWe define three complementary measures: (1)~\\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{mag}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta)) - \\tau| > \\epsilon\\}$---analogous to E-values; (2)~\\textbf{Significance ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sig}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\text{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta))| < z_{\\alpha/2} \\cdot SE(N)\\}$---relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} = \\min\\{\\delta : \\text{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\text{sign}(\\tau)\\}$---exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity---$K{=}2$]\n\\label{thm:monotone}\nFor $K{=}2$ confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$ for some scalar $\\lambda$) under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$ with $(C_0)_{jj} \\geq \\tfrac{1}{2}$, $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\\delta \\in [0,1]$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe proof (Appendix~\\ref{app:monotonicity_proof},~\\ref{par:med_monotone}) exploits perspective function convexity; $(C_0)_{jj}{\\geq}\\tfrac{1}{2}$ excludes degenerate matrices. Proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$) holds when a single latent factor drives both paths. The assumption is testable by regressing $f(A)$ on $g(A)$; it is plausible in NLP tasks with a single quality dimension but weakens under heterogeneous quality (Table~\\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}). Under NLP-realistic DGPs, the out-of-bound rate drops to 0.07\\% with zero sign failures; even under non-proportional effects with 23 real LLM matrices, sign preservation remains ${\\geq}97.3$\\% (Tables~\\ref{tab:proportional_effects},~\\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}). When $\\lambda(C) \\notin [0,1]$, Proposition~\\ref{prop:conf_gen} provides a complementary bound.\n\n\\begin{observation}[Envelope monotonicity---$K{\\geq}3$]\n\\label{prop:monotone_general}\nFor $K{\\geq}3$ under the same conditions, numerical verification across 20{,}000 random $(C_0,p,g)$ triples per $K$ shows worst-case deviations are negligible ($\\leq 0.013$; Table~\\ref{tab:conjecture_verify} in Appendix~\\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}).\n\\end{observation}\n\n\\begin{conjecture}[$\\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]\n\\label{conj:monotone}\nFor non-IV topologies under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is monotonic up to $\\varepsilon < 0.06$ deviations under default DGP parameters (collider/M-bias non-increasing, others non-decreasing; see Appendix~\\ref{sec:conjecture_verify} for DGP-dependent bounds). Verified across 10{,}000 random matrices per topology for each $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (210{,}000 total), $\\tau \\in \\{0.1, 0.5, 1.0, 2.0, 5.0\\}$.\n\\end{conjecture}\n\n\\begin{remark}[Structural distinctions from E-values]\n\\label{rem:evalue}\nASV diagnostics differ from E-values~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} in three respects (Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_comparison}, Appendix):\n\\emph{(i)~Topology dependence}: the same $C$ preserves sign under confounding but reverses it under exposure (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}); E-values treat all confounding identically.\n\\emph{(ii)~Sign-flip diagnostic}: sign-flip ASV $\\delta^*_{\\mathrm{sf}}$ has no E-value counterpart.\n\\emph{(iii)~Parametric structure}: ASV reduces $K^2$ dimensions to one via $C(\\delta)$, but $B(\\delta)$ is topology-specific and can be non-monotone (IV). E-values optimize over $[1,\\infty)$; ASVs search $\\Delta_{K-1}^K$---the space of column-stochastic matrices---a $(K{-}1)^2$-dimensional convex body whose structure makes optimization harder as $K$ grows.\n\\end{remark}\n\nUnder alternating-sign effects ($\\boldsymbol{\\beta}{=}[0,\\,1.0,\\,-0.5]$), magnitude ASV is 0.09 for exposure, 0.20 for IV, 0.35 for confounding, and $\\infty$ for M-bias (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_stability})---the same annotator quality produces a $4{\\times}$ difference in tolerable error, determined entirely by DAG topology. This topology-dependent diagnostic is unavailable in E-value frameworks, which treat all confounding structures identically.\n\nFor $K{=}2$, Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone} guarantees exact monotonicity; for $K{\\geq}3$, monotonicity rests on numerical evidence (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Sign-flip ASV $< 1$ flags sign-reversal-prone pairs; magnitude ASV $< \\delta_{\\text{observed}}$ indicates bias exceeds tolerance (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction}). The safe/fragile partition is stable across DGP parameterizations; within-group ordering depends on coefficient structure (Appendix~\\ref{sec:asv_stability}). The worst-case monotonicity deviation ($\\varepsilon \\leq 0.013$; Table~\\ref{tab:conjecture_verify}) is two orders of magnitude smaller than inter-topology ASV gaps (spanning 0.09 to $\\infty$), ensuring that the vulnerability ranking is robust to approximate monotonicity.\n",
```
