```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three NLP tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	For each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$.
10	
11	\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}
12	\label{sec:taxonomy_results}
13	
14	\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}
15	Analytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with grand MAPE of 1.6\% across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\times$ 7 topologies). Per-topology MAPE: 0.3\% (confounding) to 3.7\% (IV, reflecting Wald estimator variance amplification).
16	
17	\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}
18	Under realistic LLM accuracy (diagonal $\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\% with 2.1\% sign reversal; even at diagonal $\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\%. Under the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, 85\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee, and 83.4\% cause sign reversal of at least one coefficient. The $K{=}2$ control confirms that all tested binary matrices maintain classical attenuation~\cite{brenner1993}, establishing that the multi-class failure is structurally distinct.
19	
20	\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}
21	Five topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\% amplification~\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).
22	
23	\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}
24	The IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.8$) reduces but does not eliminate IV reversal (Appendix~\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal. Practitioners may consider bounds approaches~\cite{manski1990} or two-sample designs where the instrument is measured without error.
25	
26	\begin{table}[t]
27	\centering
28	\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\hat{\tau}| > |\tau|$. $\tau_{\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\% amplification reflects Berkson bias---misclassification is protective.}
29	\label{tab:taxonomy}
30	\small
31	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
32	\toprule
33	\textbf{Topology} & \textbf{Violation} & \textbf{Sign Flip} & \textbf{Amplif.} & $\tau_{\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\
34	\midrule
35	Confounding & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
36	Exposure & 85\% & 83.4\% & 14.1\% & No \\
37	Mediation & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
38	Front-door & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
39	Collider & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
40	M-bias & 53.2\% & 0\% & 10.4\% & Yes \\
41	IV & 100\% & 52.6\% & 100\% & No \\
42	\bottomrule
43	\end{tabular}
44	\end{table}
45	
46	The bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.
47	
48	\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}
49	\label{sec:asv_results}
50	
51	\begin{figure}[t]
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}
54	\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$).}
55	\label{fig:asv_ranking}
56	\end{figure}
57	
58	\paragraph{ASV results.}
59	The ASV envelope is $\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\varepsilon < 0.06$ under default DGP; Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\text{ASV} = \infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\% of exposure matrices yield $\delta^*_{\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\ref{tab:asv_grid}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.
60	
61	\subsection{Empirical Validation}
62	\label{sec:empirical}
63	
64	We validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$), with four LLMs producing 23 configurations under zero-shot and few-shot prompting (161 bias scenarios).
65	
66	\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}
67	HumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\%. Across 56 HumAID scenarios, $F_1$ and $|\text{bias}|$ show no significant rank correlation (Spearman $\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}). Of $K{-}1$ exposure coefficients, 14/23 configurations (61\%) exhibit sign reversal---all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.
68	
69	\begin{figure}[t]
70	\centering
71	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}
72	\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\times}$ (IV 42.1\% vs.\ collider $<$5.6\%).}
73	\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}
74	\end{figure}
75	
76	
77	\subsection{Correction Guidance}
78	\label{sec:correction}
79	
80	We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations; full results in Table~\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary}).
81	
82	\paragraph{FWL invariance.}
83	Under linear OLS, FWL implies that any linear transformation of $\mathbf{D}^*$ preserves the column space relative to $T$ for the four covariate topologies, so naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\% bias reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:fwl_proof}); exposure and IV are excluded. Under logistic regression the invariance breaks ($|\Delta\,\text{SF}_\tau| < 0.02$; Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp},~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).
84	
85	\paragraph{Method ranking.}
86	At $K{=}3$, PPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean RMSE reduction (47.0\%) but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\%): misclassification attenuates the spurious M-bias path, and PPI++ removes this protective attenuation. PPI++ also degrades with $K$, becoming harmful for exposure at $K{=}10$. MC-SIMEX is more $K$-robust (27--70\% reduction across $K{=}3,5,10$) and gold-free, though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\%). Rankings are stable across $K$ (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.80$; Appendix~\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}); no single method dominates.
87	
88	\paragraph{Method selection criteria.}
89	Covariate topology $\to$ no matrix correction (FWL); exposure/IV with gold labels $\to$ PPI++ (varies with gold fraction; Appendix~\ref{sec:ppi_gold_ratio}); otherwise $\to$ MC-SIMEX (Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}).
90	
91	\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}
92	ClinicalBERT validation confirms FWL invariance in clinical NLP (Appendix~\ref{app:case_study}). PPI++ requires ${\geq}20\%$ gold for IV but harms M-bias ($-125\%$ at 5\% gold; Appendix~\ref{sec:ppi_gold_ratio}). Under logistic regression, all structural patterns---sign preservation, topology ranking, FWL invariance breakdown---persist with deviations $< 0.02$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp},~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).
93
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: the binary attenuation guarantee breaks at $K \geq 3$, where exposure and IV can reverse signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs yield a vulnerability ranking from IV (most fragile) to M-bias (immune); matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies due to FWL invariance. Code and data will be released.
5	
6	\section*{Limitations}
7	
8	Our framework assumes non-differential misclassification, which holds when the LLM classifies without observing $Y$ but may be violated when pretraining data encodes $A$--$Y$ associations (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}). Closed-form bias expressions assume $K{=}3$ linear SEMs; extensions to arbitrary $K$ and nonlinear models~\cite{schennach2020mismeasured} remain open---proving $K{\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity remains the primary open theoretical question---though rankings are stable across $K{=}3,5,10$. The framework assumes $C$ is known or estimable from held-out gold labels. Our experiments verify formulas via Monte Carlo simulation; end-to-end demonstration with ground-truth causal effects remains future work.
9	
```

```text
3. The Related Work section is three dense paragraphs with only paragraph headings for structure, which makes it harder to scan.

Let me check what tables appear in the main text versus the appendix, then I can formulate concrete recommendations for the Director. The core insight here is that the paper's strongest contribution—showing how the same confusion matrix produces different biases across different topologies—gets buried in the presentation. I need to identify specific, actionable ways to surface this finding more prominently.

The paper tries to be three things at once: a methods paper (ASV), a benchmarking paper (correction methods), and an analytical taxonomy. It should commit to being an analytical taxonomy paper, with ASV and the correction methods serving as supporting evidence for why the taxonomy matters. The literature gap statement in Introduction paragraph 3 is critical but gets lost—it should come right after the structural break to make the contribution immediately clear.

For experiments, moving Algorithm 1 from the appendix to the main text would be the single biggest improvement since it's the practical payoff of the entire analysis. A small comparison table in Related Work showing which prior work covers which dimensions would also help readers quickly grasp what's novel. The Remark 1 about E-value comparison is currently embedded in running text... The notation section in Method 3.1 reads too much like a textbook reference—it belongs in a footnote or appendix instead. The cross-K robustness results for K=5 and K=7 keep getting mentioned but never actually appear in the main text, so I should add one concrete sentence with those numbers to back up the claim. Table 1's caption is bloated at four lines and needs trimming. And those "Numerical Verification 1" and "Numerical Verification 2" section headers feel like they were pulled straight from the appendix—they're disrupting the flow of the Method section.
```

```text
846	\caption{Correction method selection for LLM-annotated causal inference. Assumes linear structural equations; FWL-based routing may not apply under nonlinear models (see Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp}). Thresholds calibrated for $K{=}3$ linear SEM; recalibrate for different $K$ using topology-specific bias functions.}
847	\label{alg:correction}
848	\small
849	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
850	\REQUIRE DAG topology $\mathcal{T}$, gold-label availability $G \in \{\textsc{yes}, \textsc{no}\}$
851	\ENSURE Recommended method $M$, risk level $R$
852	\STATE
853	\STATE \textbf{Step 0: DAG uncertainty} \COMMENT{If $\mathcal{T}$ is uncertain, repeat Steps 1--3 under each candidate topology and report the range}
854	\STATE
855	\STATE \textbf{Step 1: Assess risk level}
856	\IF{$\mathcal{T} \in \{\text{M-bias}\}$}
857	    \STATE $R \gets \textsc{Low}$ \COMMENT{$|\text{bias}| < 0.04$ for all $C$}
858	\ELSIF{$\mathcal{T} \in \{\text{Conf., Med., Coll., Front-door}\}$}
859	    \STATE $R \gets \textsc{Medium}$ \COMMENT{bounded bias, no sign reversal}
860	\ELSE
861	    \STATE $R \gets \textsc{High}$ \COMMENT{Exposure, IV: sign reversal 83.4\% / 52.6\%}
862	\ENDIF
863	\STATE

866	    \IF{$\mathcal{T} = \text{M-bias}$}
867	        \STATE $M \gets \textsc{None}$ \COMMENT{PPI++ RMSE red.\ $-$88.0\%; no correction preferred}
```

投稿目标：EMNLP 2026 ARR Long Paper，DDL 5/25。
核心 claim：K≥3 annotation error 在不同 DAG topology 下行为质性不同（sign preservation vs reversal）。
实验完成度：~95%（6 个新实验正在并行跑，结果即将到来）。
引用不需要验证（刚完成过一轮）。

## 任务清单

**1. Algorithm 1 移入正文 §4.5**（最高优先）
- 当前 Algorithm 1 在 Appendix ~p.30
- 移入 experiments.tex §4.5 Correction Guidance，替代当前 "Method selection criteria" 段落（L88-89）
- Algorithm 占 ~0.4 列，需要通过压缩其他内容来腾空间
- 注意：如果放不下，可以做成 inline 3-step procedure 而非 algorithm float

- introduction.tex 的 3 个 contribution：
  - #1 过载（7 topologies + prove/verify/show 三层 + binarization）→ 主句只保留 taxonomy + sign preservation/reversal 二分法
  - #2 和 #3 末尾各加半句 practitioner implication：
    - ASV → "enabling practitioners to compute topology-specific error tolerance before running analyses"
    - FWL/Correction → "meaning the dominant correction paradigm fails silently for most causal designs"

**3. Conclusion 扩展**
- 当前 2 句（conclusion.tex L4）→ 扩展到 4-5 句
- 加 practical takeaway："Practitioners using LLM annotations should identify their DAG topology before choosing a correction strategy"
- 加 open question："Proving K≥3 monotonicity and extending to nonlinear models are the primary open directions"

- 如果超页，优先压缩 Related Work 或 §4.2 的冗余描述

```text
848	\small
849	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
850	\REQUIRE DAG topology $\mathcal{T}$, gold-label availability $G \in \{\textsc{yes}, \textsc{no}\}$
851	\ENSURE Recommended method $M$, risk level $R$
852	\STATE
853	\STATE \textbf{Step 0: DAG uncertainty} \COMMENT{If $\mathcal{T}$ is uncertain, repeat Steps 1--3 under each candidate topology and report the range}
854	\STATE
855	\STATE \textbf{Step 1: Assess risk level}
856	\IF{$\mathcal{T} \in \{\text{M-bias}\}$}
857	    \STATE $R \gets \textsc{Low}$ \COMMENT{$|\text{bias}| < 0.04$ for all $C$}
858	\ELSIF{$\mathcal{T} \in \{\text{Conf., Med., Coll., Front-door}\}$}
859	    \STATE $R \gets \textsc{Medium}$ \COMMENT{bounded bias, no sign reversal}
860	\ELSE
861	    \STATE $R \gets \textsc{High}$ \COMMENT{Exposure, IV: sign reversal 83.4\% / 52.6\%}
862	\ENDIF
863	\STATE
864	\STATE \textbf{Step 2: Select correction method}
865	\IF{$G = \textsc{yes}$}
866	    \IF{$\mathcal{T} = \text{M-bias}$}
867	        \STATE $M \gets \textsc{None}$ \COMMENT{PPI++ RMSE red.\ $-$88.0\%; no correction preferred}
868	    \ELSE
869	        \STATE $M \gets \text{PPI++}$ \COMMENT{47.0\% RMSE red., 96.8\% coverage}
870	    \ENDIF
871	\ELSE
872	    \STATE \COMMENT{no gold labels}
873	    \IF{$\mathcal{T} \in \{\text{Confounding, Mediation}\}$}
874	        \STATE $M \gets \text{DSL}$ \COMMENT{48.0\% / 52.9\% RMSE reduction}
875	    \ELSIF{$\mathcal{T} = \text{IV}$}
876	        \STATE $M \gets \text{MC-SIMEX}$ \COMMENT{59.4\% RMSE reduction}
877	    \ELSIF{$\mathcal{T} \in \{\text{Collider, M-bias}\}$}
878	        \STATE $M \gets \text{MC-SIMEX}$ \COMMENT{31.8\% / 0.1\% RMSE reduction}
879	    \ELSE
880	        \STATE $M \gets \emptyset$; flag: acquire gold labels \COMMENT{no reliable gold-free method}
881	    \ENDIF
882	\ENDIF
883	\STATE
884	\STATE \textbf{Step 3: FWL invariance warning}
885	\IF{$\mathcal{T} \in \{\text{Conf., Med., Coll., M-bias}\}$}
886	    \STATE Skip Naive plug-in and MLA \COMMENT{0\% bias red.\ on $T$ coeff.\ (Table~\ref{tab:correction}: below 2\%, no method improves)}
887	\ENDIF
888	\RETURN $(M, R)$
889	\end{algorithmic}
890	\end{algorithm}
891	
892	\subsection{Proof of FWL Invariance}
893	\label{app:fwl_proof}
894	
```

```json
    "oldString": "\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification in linear structural equation models, prove sign preservation for confounding and collider, verify it numerically for mediation, M-bias, and front-door, and show that exposure and IV can produce sign reversal. Binarization amplifies the gap: collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary introduces additional bias varying $154{\\times}$ across topologies (Appendix~\\ref{app:binarization}).",
    "newString": "\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification, establishing a sign-preservation/reversal dichotomy: five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign, while exposure and IV can reverse it (binarization amplifies this gap $154{\\times}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:binarization}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors (equivalently, misclassification) from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\\% for smaller models; these errors are highly correlated across models (60\\% agreement when both err)~\\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \\emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.\n\nThe transition at $K{\\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with positive diagonal to preserve coefficient signs---an algebraic property of the $2{\\times}2$ matrix dimension~\\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee \\emph{vanishes} at $K{\\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal even under high accuracy~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. Under a uniform Dirichlet prior, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient; under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), 2.1\\% still do. The primary treatment coefficient, however, is never reversed in any tested configuration---an empirical regularity we discuss in Section~\\ref{sec:method}.\n\nStandard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\\% to 42.1\\%). $F_1$ shows no significant rank correlation with causal bias across 56 HumAID scenarios (Spearman $\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$), confirming that \\emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \\citet{dosemeci1990} and \\citet{veirod_laake2001} noted the possibility of polytomous sign reversal; \\citet{brenner1993} identified amplification for polytomous confounders; \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology. We present the first unified analysis of \\emph{LLM-generated} multi-class annotation error propagation across seven canonical DAG topologies \\emph{in linear SEMs}, extending prior single-topology analyses~\\cite{brenner1993, teblunthuis2024}. Our analytical predictions match Monte Carlo estimates within 1.6\\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification in linear structural equation models, prove sign preservation for confounding and collider, verify it numerically for mediation, M-bias, and front-door, and show that exposure and IV can produce sign reversal. Binarization amplifies the gap: collapsing $K{=}3$ to binary introduces additional bias varying $154{\\times}$ across topologies (Appendix~\\ref{app:binarization}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} As a corollary of the bias taxonomy, we define three complementary ASV diagnostics---magnitude, significance, and sign-flip---revealing a topology-dependent vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile; 52.6\\% sign reversal under random Dirichlet prior, though zero reversals occur under empirical LLM matrices) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance} (a systematic benchmark, not a new correction method). We evaluate five correction methods and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate---a negative result overlooked in the annotation correction literature. PPI++ achieves 47.0\\% RMSE reduction but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\\%).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
```

```json
    "newString": "\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate, meaning the dominant correction paradigm (confusion-matrix inversion) fails silently for most causal designs. PPI++ achieves 47.0\\% RMSE reduction but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\\%).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors (equivalently, misclassification) from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\\% for smaller models; these errors are highly correlated across models (60\\% agreement when both err)~\\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \\emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.\n\nThe transition at $K{\\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with positive diagonal to preserve coefficient signs---an algebraic property of the $2{\\times}2$ matrix dimension~\\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee \\emph{vanishes} at $K{\\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal even under high accuracy~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. Under a uniform Dirichlet prior, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient; under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), 2.1\\% still do. The primary treatment coefficient, however, is never reversed in any tested configuration---an empirical regularity we discuss in Section~\\ref{sec:method}.\n\nStandard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\\% to 42.1\\%). $F_1$ shows no significant rank correlation with causal bias across 56 HumAID scenarios (Spearman $\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$), confirming that \\emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \\citet{dosemeci1990} and \\citet{veirod_laake2001} noted the possibility of polytomous sign reversal; \\citet{brenner1993} identified amplification for polytomous confounders; \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology. We present the first unified analysis of \\emph{LLM-generated} multi-class annotation error propagation across seven canonical DAG topologies \\emph{in linear SEMs}, extending prior single-topology analyses~\\cite{brenner1993, teblunthuis2024}. Our analytical predictions match Monte Carlo estimates within 1.6\\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification, establishing a sign-preservation/reversal dichotomy: five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign, while exposure and IV can reverse it (binarization amplifies this gap $154{\\times}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:binarization}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} As a corollary of the bias taxonomy, we define three complementary ASV diagnostics---magnitude, significance, and sign-flip---revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$), enabling practitioners to compute topology-specific error tolerance before running analyses.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance} (a systematic benchmark, not a new correction method). We evaluate five correction methods and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\\% bias reduction for four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate---a negative result overlooked in the annotation correction literature. PPI++ achieves 47.0\\% RMSE reduction but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\\%).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: the binary attenuation guarantee breaks at $K \geq 3$, where exposure and IV can reverse signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs yield a vulnerability ranking from IV (most fragile) to M-bias (immune); matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies due to FWL invariance.
5	
6	\section*{Limitations}
7	
8	We assume non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}). Bias expressions assume $K{=}3$ linear SEMs; proving $K{\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity remains the primary open question~\cite{schennach2020mismeasured}, though rankings are stable across $K{=}3,5,10$. The framework requires estimable $C$; experiments are Monte Carlo without end-to-end causal benchmarks.
9	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0127Hx8J5cHinMo9fPqoojKM`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three NLP tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).
5	
6	\subsection{Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	For each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$.
10	
11	\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}
12	\label{sec:taxonomy_results}
13	
14	\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}
15	Analytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with grand MAPE of 1.6\% across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\%--3.7\%).
16	
17	\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}
18	Under realistic LLM accuracy (diagonal $\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\% with 2.1\% sign reversal; even at diagonal $\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\%. Under the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, 85\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee, and 83.4\% cause sign reversal of at least one coefficient. The $K{=}2$ control confirms that all tested binary matrices maintain classical attenuation~\cite{brenner1993}, establishing that the multi-class failure is structurally distinct.
19	
20	\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}
21	Five topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\% amplification~\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).
22	
23	\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}
24	The IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.8$) reduces but does not eliminate IV reversal (Appendix~\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.
25	
26	\begin{table}[t]
27	\centering
28	\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\hat{\tau}| > |\tau|$. $\tau_{\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\% amplification reflects Berkson bias---misclassification is protective.}
29	\label{tab:taxonomy}
30	\small
31	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
32	\toprule
33	\textbf{Topology} & \textbf{Violation} & \textbf{Sign Flip} & \textbf{Amplif.} & $\tau_{\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\
34	\midrule
35	Confounding & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
36	Exposure & 85\% & 83.4\% & 14.1\% & No \\
37	Mediation & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
38	Front-door & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
39	Collider & 100\% & 0\% & 100\% & Cond. \\
40	M-bias & 53.2\% & 0\% & 10.4\% & Yes \\
41	IV & 100\% & 52.6\% & 100\% & No \\
42	\bottomrule
43	\end{tabular}
44	\end{table}
45	
46	The bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.
47	
48	\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}
49	\label{sec:asv_results}
50	
51	\begin{figure}[t]
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}
54	\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$).}
55	\label{fig:asv_ranking}
56	\end{figure}
57	
58	\paragraph{ASV results.}
59	The ASV envelope is $\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\varepsilon < 0.008$ for non-exposure, $\varepsilon \approx 0.075$ for exposure; Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\text{ASV} = \infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\% of exposure matrices yield $\delta^*_{\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\ref{tab:asv_grid}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.
60	
61	\subsection{Empirical Validation}
62	\label{sec:empirical}
63	
64	We validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$), with four LLMs producing 23 configurations under zero-shot and few-shot prompting (161 bias scenarios).
65	
66	\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}
67	HumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\%. Across 56 HumAID scenarios, $F_1$ and $|\text{bias}|$ show no significant rank correlation (Spearman $\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}). Of $K{-}1$ exposure coefficients, 14/23 configurations (61\%) exhibit sign reversal---all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.
68	
69	\begin{figure}[t]
70	\centering
71	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}
72	\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\times}$ (IV 42.1\% vs.\ collider $<$5.6\%).}
73	\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}
74	\end{figure}
75	
76	
77	\subsection{Correction Guidance}
78	\label{sec:correction}
79	
80	We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations; full results in Table~\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary}).
81	
82	\paragraph{FWL invariance.}
83	Under linear OLS, FWL implies that any linear transformation of $\mathbf{D}^*$ preserves the column space relative to $T$ for the four covariate topologies, so naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\% bias reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:fwl_proof}); exposure and IV are excluded. Under logistic regression the invariance breaks ($|\Delta\,\text{SF}_\tau| < 0.02$; Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp},~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).
84	
85	\paragraph{Method ranking.}
86	At $K{=}3$, PPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean RMSE reduction (47.0\%) but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\%): misclassification attenuates the spurious M-bias path, and PPI++ removes this protective attenuation. MC-SIMEX is more $K$-robust (27--70\% reduction across $K{=}3,5,10$) and gold-free, though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\%). Rankings are stable across $K$ (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.80$; Appendix~\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).
87	
88	\paragraph{Method selection criteria.}
89	Covariate topology $\to$ no matrix correction (FWL); exposure/IV with gold labels $\to$ PPI++ (varies with gold fraction; Appendix~\ref{sec:ppi_gold_ratio}); otherwise $\to$ MC-SIMEX (Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}).
90	
91	\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}
92	ClinicalBERT validation confirms FWL invariance in clinical NLP (Appendix~\ref{app:case_study}). PPI++ requires ${\geq}20\%$ gold for IV but harms M-bias ($-125\%$ at 5\% gold; Appendix~\ref{sec:ppi_gold_ratio}). Under logistic regression, all structural patterns---sign preservation, topology ranking, FWL invariance breakdown---persist with deviations $< 0.02$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp},~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).
93	
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three NLP tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with grand MAPE of 1.6\\% across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic LLM accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; even at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\%. Under the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, 85\\% of $K{=}3$ confusion matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee, and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal of at least one coefficient. The $K{=}2$ control confirms that all tested binary matrices maintain classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing that the multi-class failure is structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.8$) reduces but does not eliminate IV reversal (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias---misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}\n\\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).}\n\\label{fig:asv_ranking}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\\varepsilon < 0.008$ for non-exposure, $\\varepsilon \\approx 0.075$ for exposure; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_grid}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$), with four LLMs producing 23 configurations under zero-shot and few-shot prompting (161 bias scenarios).\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}\nHumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. Across 56 HumAID scenarios, $F_1$ and $|\\text{bias}|$ show no significant rank correlation (Spearman $\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}). Of $K{-}1$ exposure coefficients, 14/23 configurations (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal---all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations; full results in Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}).\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear OLS, FWL implies that any linear transformation of $\\mathbf{D}^*$ preserves the column space relative to $T$ for the four covariate topologies, so naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\\% bias reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}); exposure and IV are excluded. ```text
```
```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Large language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science, with researchers using LLM-generated labels for tasks ranging from sentiment classification to political stance detection~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Crucially, most of these tasks are inherently multi-class: stance detection uses 3--4 categories, sentiment analysis 5, and disaster response taxonomies 10 or more. This shift promises dramatic cost reduction, but introduces a critical validity gap: annotation errors (equivalently, misclassification) from imperfect classifiers propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both the error structure and the causal design. \citet{baumann2025} demonstrate that across 37 annotation tasks, 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, rising to 50\% for smaller models; these errors are highly correlated across models (60\% agreement when both err)~\cite{kim_garg2025}, undermining ensemble-based mitigation. Analyzing the \emph{structure} of annotation error, rather than simply reducing its magnitude, is essential for valid downstream inference.
5	
6	The transition at $K{\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with positive diagonal to preserve coefficient signs---an algebraic property of the $2{\times}2$ matrix dimension~\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee \emph{vanishes} at $K{\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients, enabling sign reversal even under high accuracy~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. Under a uniform Dirichlet prior, 83.4\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient; under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\geq 0.7$), 2.1\% still do. The primary treatment coefficient, however, is never reversed in any tested configuration---an empirical regularity we discuss in Section~\ref{sec:method}.
7	
8	Standard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\% to 42.1\%). $F_1$ shows no significant rank correlation with causal bias across 56 HumAID scenarios (Spearman $\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$), confirming that \emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \citet{dosemeci1990} and \citet{veirod_laake2001} noted the possibility of polytomous sign reversal; \citet{brenner1993} identified amplification for polytomous confounders; \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology. We present the first unified analysis of \emph{LLM-generated} multi-class annotation error propagation across seven canonical DAG topologies \emph{in linear SEMs}, extending prior single-topology analyses~\cite{brenner1993, teblunthuis2024}. Our analytical predictions match Monte Carlo estimates within 1.6\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.
9	
10	We address these gaps with three contributions:
11	
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification, establishing a sign-preservation/reversal dichotomy: five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign, while exposure and IV can reverse it (binarization amplifies this gap $154{\times}$; Appendix~\ref{app:binarization}).
14	
15	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} As a corollary of the bias taxonomy, we define three complementary ASV diagnostics---magnitude, significance, and sign-flip---revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$), enabling practitioners to compute topology-specific error tolerance before running analyses.
16	
17	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction for four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate, meaning the dominant correction paradigm (confusion-matrix inversion) fails silently for most causal designs. PPI++ achieves 47.0\% RMSE reduction but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\%).
18	\end{enumerate}
19	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011x6o8k9upzz2pDSqw7SShu`</summary>

```text
7	
8	Standard classification metrics do not predict downstream causal risk: within a narrow $F_1$ range of 0.614--0.690 across three frontier LLMs, relative bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies (from 4.1\% to 42.1\%). $F_1$ shows no significant rank correlation with causal bias across 56 HumAID scenarios (Spearman $\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$), confirming that \emph{topology}, not annotator quality, determines downstream risk. This fundamental asymmetry has received limited systematic treatment. \citet{dosemeci1990} and \citet{veirod_laake2001} noted the possibility of polytomous sign reversal; \citet{brenner1993} identified amplification for polytomous confounders; \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended classifier error analysis to categorical variables and LLM classifiers, but covered only one DAG topology. We present the first unified analysis of \emph{LLM-generated} multi-class annotation error propagation across seven canonical DAG topologies \emph{under structural equation models}, extending prior single-topology analyses~\cite{brenner1993, teblunthuis2024}. Our analytical predictions match Monte Carlo estimates within 1.6\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.
9	
10	We address these gaps with three contributions:
11	
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification, establishing a sign-preservation/reversal dichotomy: five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign, while exposure and IV can reverse it (binarization amplifies this gap $154{\times}$; Appendix~\ref{app:binarization}).
14	
15	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} As a corollary of the bias taxonomy, we define three complementary ASV diagnostics---magnitude, significance, and sign-flip---revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \infty$), enabling practitioners to compute topology-specific error tolerance before running analyses.
16	
17	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction guidance.} We evaluate five correction methods and prove a Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction for four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate, meaning the dominant correction paradigm (confusion-matrix inversion) fails silently for most causal designs. PPI++ achieves 47.0\% RMSE reduction but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\%).
18	\end{enumerate}
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: the binary attenuation guarantee breaks at $K \geq 3$, where exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs yield a vulnerability ranking from IV (most fragile) to M-bias (immune); matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies due to FWL invariance. Practitioners using LLM annotations in causal designs should identify their DAG topology and compute topology-specific ASV before choosing a correction strategy---$F_1$ alone does not predict downstream risk. Proving envelope monotonicity for general $K$ and extending the framework to nonlinear models and differential misclassification are the primary open directions. Code and data will be released.
5	
6	\section*{Limitations}
7	
8	We assume non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}). Bias expressions assume $K{=}3$ linear SEMs; proving $K{\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity remains the primary open question~\cite{schennach2020mismeasured}, though rankings are stable across $K{=}3,5,10$. The framework requires estimable $C$; experiments are Monte Carlo without end-to-end causal benchmarks.
9	
```

```text
87	
88	\paragraph{Correction decision procedure.}
89	Given DAG topology $\mathcal{T}$ and gold-label availability: (1)~\textbf{Risk:} M-bias $\to$ low; confounding, mediation, collider, front-door $\to$ medium (no sign reversal); exposure, IV $\to$ high. (2)~\textbf{Method:} with gold labels, use PPI++ (47.0\% RMSE reduction) except M-bias (harmful, $-$88\%); without gold, DSL for confounding/mediation, MC-SIMEX for IV/collider, otherwise acquire gold. (3)~\textbf{FWL warning:} skip naive plug-in and MLA for covariate topologies (0\% bias reduction). If $\mathcal{T}$ is uncertain, repeat under candidate topologies (full pseudocode: Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}, Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary}).
90	
91	\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}
92	ClinicalBERT validation confirms FWL invariance in clinical NLP (Appendix~\ref{app:case_study}). PPI++ requires ${\geq}20\%$ gold for IV but harms M-bias ($-125\%$ at 5\% gold; Appendix~\ref{sec:ppi_gold_ratio}). Under logistic regression, all structural patterns---sign preservation, topology ranking, FWL invariance breakdown---persist with deviations $< 0.02$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp},~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).
```

E-values are topology-agnostic.\n\\emph{(ii)~Sign-flip diagnostic}: sign-flip ASV $\\delta^*_{\\mathrm{sf}}$ targets a failure mode absent from unmeasured confounding.\n\\emph{(iii)~Parametric structure}: ASV searches $C(\\delta)$ paths in the column-stochastic polytope; E-values optimize over $[1,\\infty)$.\n\\end{remark}\n```\n\n### P2: SF-9 Conjecture 1 ε 理论直觉\n\n**文件**: method.tex，在 Conjecture 1 后（L105 附近）加 1-2 句\n**增加**：\n```\nIntuitively, $\\varepsilon$ is small because under proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$), envelope monotonicity holds exactly (Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}); Under logistic regression---the dominant model in NLP classification---FWL invariance breaks: matrix corrections now achieve non-zero bias reduction, with breakdown magnitude increasing with classifier accuracy (exposure: $-22$\% to $-78$\%; Appendix~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}). The safe/fragile partition and topology ranking are preserved ($|\Delta\,\text{SF}_\tau| < 0.02$; Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp}), but practitioners should use topology-aware correction under logistic models rather than assuming covariate-topology immunity.

参考改写：
```
\item \textbf{Correction guidance.} As a practical implication, we evaluate five correction methods and prove an FWL invariance result: matrix-based corrections achieve exactly 0\% bias reduction for four covariate topologies, meaning confusion-matrix inversion fails silently for most causal designs.
```

### P2: SF-6 ASV vs E-value 重新框定

**文件**: method.tex L107-113 (Remark 1)
**修改**：从 "structural distinctions" 改为 "complementary tools"。去掉暗示 ASV 优于 E-value 的语气。

参考改写：
```
\begin{remark}[ASV and E-values as complementary tools]
\label{rem:evalue}
ASV diagnostics and E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} address different bias sources---annotation error vs.\ unmeasured confounding---and are most valuable when used jointly (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_comparison}, Appendix).
\emph{(i)~Topology dependence}: ASV captures how the same $C$ has different consequences across topologies; E-values are topology-agnostic.
\emph{(ii)~Sign-flip diagnostic}: sign-flip ASV $\delta^*_{\mathrm{sf}}$ targets a failure mode absent from unmeasured confounding.
\emph{(iii)~Parametric structure}: ASV searches $C(\delta)$ paths in the column-stochastic polytope; E-values optimize over $[1,\infty)$.
\end{remark}
```

### P2: SF-9 Conjecture 1 ε 理论直觉

```
Intuitively, $\varepsilon$ is small because under proportional effects ($f = \lambda g$), envelope monotonicity holds exactly (Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}); deviations arise from non-proportional residuals, whose magnitude is bounded by the convex geometry of the column-stochastic matrix space---perturbations along $C(\delta)$ traverse a convex path that limits how far the bias function can deviate from monotonicity.
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup and Notation}
5	\label{sec:notation}
6	
7	Let $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one. Following \citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.
8	
9	\paragraph{Notation conventions.}
10	Our analysis characterizes systematic bias---the value an estimator converges to as $N \to \infty$ (the probability limit, or plim)---rather than finite-sample variance. The Frisch--Waugh--Lovell (FWL) theorem guarantees that each regression coefficient equals the coefficient from a simple regression after residualizing all other variables; this is why matrix-based corrections fail for covariate topologies (Section~\ref{sec:correction}): replacing the mismeasured variable with a corrected version does not change the residualized relationship. The Wald estimator (ratio of reduced-form to first-stage effects) diverges under weak instruments; Dirichlet priors over probability vectors represent uncertainty over misclassification patterns. Table~\ref{tab:notation} (Appendix~\ref{app:notation_table}) collects all notation.
11	
12	\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]
13	\label{def:nondiff}
14	Misclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k, Y, \mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.
15	\end{definition}
16	
17	This holds when the LLM classifies without observing the outcome---the typical CSS setup~\cite{ziems2024can}; topic-stratified confusion matrices confirm cross-group stability (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_verify}). Violations arise when pretraining data encodes $A$--$Y$ associations; our stress test (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}) shows $<$3~pp degradation under uniform $\varepsilon{=}0.05$ violations, but $\varepsilon{=}0.01$ structured departure produces sign reversal in 14/16 $K{\geq}3$ exposure configurations (Appendix~\ref{sec:realistic_epsilon}). Practitioners can test this by comparing $C$ across outcome strata; in typical CSS pipelines where annotation is performed without access to $Y$, non-differentiality is structurally ensured~\cite{ziems2024can}.
18	
19	We study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\cite{pearl2009, hernan2020causal} in which $A$ occupies structurally distinct causal roles: confounder, exposure, mediator, collider, M-bias variable, front-door mediator, and instrument. Throughout, we derive probability limits under \emph{linear} structural equation models~\cite{carroll2006}, the standard framework for exact characterization of topology-dependent bias; robustness under logistic regression~\cite{stefanski_carroll1985} is verified in Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp} and~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}.
20	
```

The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one. Following \\citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \\emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.\n\n\\paragraph{Notation conventions.}\nOur analysis characterizes systematic bias---the value an estimator converges to as $N \\to \\infty$ (the probability limit, or plim)---rather than finite-sample variance. The Frisch--Waugh--Lovell (FWL) theorem guarantees that each regression coefficient equals the coefficient from a simple regression after residualizing all other variables; this is why matrix-based corrections fail for covariate topologies (Section~\\ref{sec:correction}): replacing the mismeasured variable with a corrected version does not change the residualized relationship. The Wald estimator (ratio of reduced-form to first-stage effects) diverges under weak instruments; Dirichlet priors over probability vectors represent uncertainty over misclassification patterns. Table~\\ref{tab:notation} (Appendix~\\ref{app:notation_table}) collects all notation.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]\n\\label{def:nondiff}\nMisclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k, Y, \\mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis holds when the LLM classifies without observing the outcome---the typical CSS setup~\\cite{ziems2024can}; topic-stratified confusion matrices confirm cross-group stability (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_verify}). Violations arise when pretraining data encodes $A$--$Y$ associations; our stress test (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}) shows $<$3~pp degradation under uniform $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$ violations, but $\\varepsilon{=}0.01$ structured departure produces sign reversal in 14/16 $K{\\geq}3$ exposure configurations (Appendix~\\ref{sec:realistic_epsilon}). Practitioners can test this by comparing $C$ across outcome strata; in typical CSS pipelines where annotation is performed without access to $Y$, non-differentiality is structurally ensured~\\cite{ziems2024can}.\n\nWe study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\\cite{pearl2009, hernan2020causal} in which $A$ occupies structurally distinct causal roles: confounder, exposure, mediator, collider, M-bias variable, front-door mediator, and instrument. Throughout, we derive probability limits under \\emph{linear} structural equation models~\\cite{carroll2006}, the standard framework for exact characterization of topology-dependent bias; robustness under logistic regression~\\cite{stefanski_carroll1985} is verified in Appendix~\\ref{sec:logistic_dgp} and~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}.\n",

```json
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 118
    }
```

```text
77	\subsection{Correction Guidance}
78	\label{sec:correction}
79	
80	We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all seven topologies under six error structures (210 configurations; full results in Table~\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary}).
81	
82	\paragraph{FWL invariance.}
83	Under linear OLS, FWL implies that any linear transformation of $\mathbf{D}^*$ preserves the column space relative to $T$ for the four covariate topologies, so naive plug-in and MLA achieve exactly 0.0\% bias reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:fwl_proof}); exposure and IV are excluded. Under logistic regression the invariance breaks ($|\Delta\,\text{SF}_\tau| < 0.02$; Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp},~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}).
84	
85	\paragraph{Method ranking.}
86	At $K{=}3$, PPI++ achieves the highest grand-mean RMSE reduction (47.0\%) but requires gold labels and is harmful for M-bias ($-$88\%): misclassification attenuates the spurious M-bias path, and PPI++ removes this protective attenuation. MC-SIMEX is more $K$-robust (27--70\% reduction across $K{=}3,5,10$) and gold-free, though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\%). At $K{=}10$, PPI++ becomes harmful for IV ($-$124\%) and exposure ($-$11\%), making MC-SIMEX the dominant gold-free method. Rankings are stable across $K$ (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.80$; Appendix~\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).
87	
88	\paragraph{Correction decision procedure.}
89	Given DAG topology $\mathcal{T}$ and gold-label availability: (1)~\textbf{Risk:} M-bias $\to$ low; confounding, mediation, collider, front-door $\to$ medium (no sign reversal); exposure, IV $\to$ high. (2)~\textbf{Method:} with gold labels, use PPI++ (47.0\% RMSE reduction) except M-bias (harmful, $-$88\%); without gold, DSL for confounding/mediation, MC-SIMEX for IV/collider, otherwise acquire gold. (3)~\textbf{FWL warning:} skip naive plug-in and MLA for covariate topologies (0\% bias reduction). If $\mathcal{T}$ is uncertain, repeat under candidate topologies (full pseudocode: Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}, Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary}).
90	
91	\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}
92	ClinicalBERT validation confirms FWL invariance in clinical NLP (Appendix~\ref{app:case_study}). Empirical validation on HumAID ($K{=}10$) confirms: PPI++ achieves 86\% bias reduction for confounding but becomes harmful for IV ($-$79\%; Appendix~\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). PPI++ requires ${\geq}20\%$ gold for IV but harms M-bias ($-125\%$ at 5\% gold; Appendix~\ref{sec:ppi_gold_ratio}). Under logistic regression, all structural patterns---sign preservation, topology ranking, FWL invariance breakdown---persist with deviations $< 0.02$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp},~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}). The safe/fragile partition is recoverable from $\hat{C}$ with ${\geq}100$ gold labels (82\% bootstrap stability; Appendix~\ref{sec:c_robustness}). A complete end-to-end demonstration---from $\hat{C}$ estimation through ASV diagnosis to topology-routed correction---confirms these patterns (Table~\ref{tab:e2e-demo}, Appendix~\ref{sec:e2e_demo}).
```

```text
90	\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity---$K{=}2$]
91	\label{thm:monotone}
92	For $K{=}2$ confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \lambda g$ for some scalar $\lambda$) under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$ with $(C_0)_{jj} \geq \tfrac{1}{2}$, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\delta \in [0,1]$.
93	\end{theorem}
94	
95	The proof (Appendix~\ref{app:monotonicity_proof},~\ref{par:med_monotone}) exploits perspective function convexity; $(C_0)_{jj}{\geq}\tfrac{1}{2}$ excludes degenerate matrices. Proportional effects ($f = \lambda g$) holds when a single latent factor drives both paths. The assumption is testable by regressing $f(A)$ on $g(A)$; it is plausible in NLP tasks with a single quality dimension but weakens under heterogeneous quality (Table~\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}). Under NLP-realistic DGPs, the out-of-bound rate drops to 0.07\% with zero sign failures; even under non-proportional effects with 23 real LLM matrices, sign preservation remains ${\geq}97.3$\% (Tables~\ref{tab:proportional_effects},~\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}). When $\lambda(C) \notin [0,1]$, Proposition~\ref{prop:conf_gen} provides a complementary bound.
96	
97	\begin{observation}[Envelope monotonicity---$K{\geq}3$]
98	\label{prop:monotone_general}
99	For $K{\geq}3$ under the same conditions, numerical verification across 20{,}000 random $(C_0,p,g)$ triples per $K$ shows worst-case deviations are small under moderate effect sizes ($\varepsilon < 0.02$ for $|\beta| \leq 1$) but scale with effect magnitude; exposure deviations are consistently largest (Table~\ref{tab:conjecture_verify}, Appendix~\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}).
100	\end{observation}
101	
102	\begin{conjecture}[$\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]
103	\label{conj:monotone}
104	For non-IV topologies under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is $\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic (collider/M-bias non-increasing, others non-decreasing) with $\varepsilon$ depending on topology and effect magnitude $|\beta|$: small under moderate effects ($\varepsilon < 0.02$ for $|\beta| \leq 1$) and scaling with $|\beta|$ (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:dgp_sensitivity}). The safe/fragile partition---five sign-preserving topologies versus exposure and IV---is robust across all tested parameterizations.
105	\end{conjecture}
106	
107	\begin{remark}[Structural distinctions from E-values]
108	\label{rem:evalue}
109	ASV diagnostics differ from E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} in three respects (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_comparison}, Appendix):
110	\emph{(i)~Topology dependence}: the same $C$ preserves sign under confounding but reverses it under exposure (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}); E-values treat all confounding identically.
111	\emph{(ii)~Sign-flip diagnostic}: sign-flip ASV $\delta^*_{\mathrm{sf}}$ has no E-value counterpart.
112	\emph{(iii)~Parametric structure}: ASV reduces $K^2$ dimensions to one via $C(\delta)$, but $B(\delta)$ is topology-specific and can be non-monotone (IV). E-values optimize over $[1,\infty)$; ASVs search $\Delta_{K-1}^K$---the space of column-stochastic matrices---a $(K{-}1)^2$-dimensional convex body whose structure makes optimization harder as $K$ grows.
113	\end{remark}
114	
115	Under alternating-sign effects ($\boldsymbol{\beta}{=}[0,\,1.0,\,-0.5]$), magnitude ASV is 0.09 for exposure, 0.20 for IV, 0.35 for confounding, and $\infty$ for M-bias (Table~\ref{tab:asv_stability})---the same annotator quality produces a $4{\times}$ difference in tolerable error, determined entirely by DAG topology. This topology-dependent diagnostic is unavailable in E-value frameworks, which treat all confounding structures identically.
116	
117	For $K{=}2$, Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone} guarantees exact monotonicity; for $K{\geq}3$, monotonicity rests on numerical evidence (Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}). Sign-flip ASV $< 1$ flags sign-reversal-prone pairs; magnitude ASV $< \delta_{\text{observed}}$ indicates bias exceeds tolerance (Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}). The safe/fragile partition is stable across DGP parameterizations; within-group ordering depends on coefficient structure (Appendix~\ref{sec:asv_stability}). Under moderate effect sizes ($|\beta| \leq 1$), the worst-case monotonicity deviation ($\varepsilon < 0.02$ for non-exposure, $\varepsilon < 0.08$ for exposure) is small relative to inter-topology ASV gaps (spanning 0.09 to $\infty$), ensuring that the vulnerability ranking is robust.
118	
```
